# Push delivery: wake listeners from Supabase Realtime instead of polling

**Status:** SPECIFICATION, draft 4 (final-round candidate), on branch `spec/push-delivery`. Assignment B from the operator via CSwarmDevLead (ask `3e9049cf`), authored by CSwarmStrategist (principal `2121f81d`). Not implemented. Nothing here is live.
**Authority:** on adoption this document becomes the §2.13 addendum of `docs/design/SWARM-CLOUD.md`; until then the spec wins on conflict.
**Scope ruling (CSwarmDevLead, 2026-09-06):** this spec covers option 3 of the brain topic `edge-function-invocations-2026-09`: wake listeners from Realtime, with a fallback poll. The one-constant change (`LISTENER_IDLE_POLL_MS` 2000→15000, `ACTIVITY_HEARTBEAT_MS` 15000→60000), the read+claim merge, the watcher dedupe, and the `file_versions` scan are **lane A** (Grok, `lane/idle-cost`) and land first. The cross-machine duplicate-listener guard is deferred. Where this spec touches those, it says so and does not redo them.
**Evidence:** `docs/evidence/2026-09-06-push-delivery-spec/` — the code map (`code-map-explore-agent.md`), CodexDesktop's client and server passes, the local Realtime experiment, the local query plans, and the review arms per SHA under `arms/`. Every file:line below was confirmed on `d500973` by the author or by a named pass.
**Draft 2 and 3 changes:** the review record (§11) lists what each round's arms found and where each item landed. The design's core has not changed across drafts; its wire contract, persistence gate, lane ownership, status shape, and arithmetic have.

## 0. The answer in one paragraph

A listener today spends its life asking "anything for me?" every two seconds, twice per tick, and the server writes three rows to say "no". Replace the question with a doorbell: when a delivery row is inserted for a principal, a Postgres trigger broadcasts a content-free **wake** on a private Realtime topic named by that principal's current **wake id**; only a client that knows the id can join, and the id changes whenever a credential of that principal is revoked by intent. The listener holds one websocket and claims on the wake. Polling stays as the safety net, not the transport: one **reconcile** (a read plus a claim) every 5 minutes while the socket is subscribed, one claim every 15 seconds while it is not. The claim path, the lease, the ack, and the receipts do not change; the wake is a latency hint and the `signal_deliveries` row stays the only truth, so a lost wake costs at most one reconcile interval and never a delivery. An empty claim stops persisting audit and idempotency rows. The same wake topic serves `inbox --notify`; a workspace-level topic serves the app's feed. Measured idle cost per seat per day falls from about 53,800 edge invocations to about 2,200; rows written per idle day fall from about 49,400 (24,000 audit rows kept forever, 24,000 idempotency rows kept 30 days) to about 400 rate-limit rows that a cron purges within two hours.

---

## 1. Stage 1 — the invocation path per idle tick today (measured)

### 1.1 One idle tick of a `durable_claim` listener

| # | call | edge | from | persists |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `readAgentSignalPage` (inbox page, limit 100) | `POST /functions/v1/read` | `src/listener/runtime.ts:1075-1082` → `src/cloud/signals.ts:1118,1140-1166` | `swarm.record_renewal_grant_use(...)` on every call (`read/index.ts:490-496`); `agent_delivery_read_context` can UPDATE `agent_tokens` in its handover CTE and runs a COUNT(*) join over deliveries×signals (`:437-465`). No audit, no idempotency row. |
| 2 | `claim_agent_inbox` (limit 1) | `POST /functions/v1/command` | `runtime.ts:1365-1394` → `src/cloud/delivery.ts:821-847` | **three rows per empty claim**: `audit_log` (`command/index.ts:8424-8432`), `idempotency_keys` (`:8365-8380`), and a `rate_buckets` upsert by `checkDeliveryRateLimit` (`:4868`, `:7533`) keyed on `date_trunc('minute')` (`:4881-4888`), so one row per principal per **minute**, purged by cron after two hours (`20260723000001:845`). Zero `signal_deliveries` rows change. |
| 3 | `sleep(LISTENER_IDLE_POLL_MS = 2_000)` | — | `runtime.ts:77`, `:778`, `:1455-1456` | — |

Measured production wall clock per tick ≈ 3.6 s (ledger, `docs/org/2026-08-29-RESUME-HERE.md:2283`), so per idle seat per day: 24,000 reads + 24,000 claims.

### 1.2 The other periodic callers of the same seat

| caller | cadence | edge | source |
|---|---|---|---|
| activity heartbeat | every 15 s idle, up to 1.33/s active | `POST /functions/v1/activity` → `realtime.send` | `src/listener/activity.ts:13`, `:98-146`; `activity/index.ts:142-152` |
| renewal | lazy, ≈ every 54 min, on the next request | `command` | `src/cloud/renewal.ts:852-853`, `:121-125` |
| `inbox --notify` watcher (separate process) | every 25 s | `read` | `src/cloud/arrival-watch.ts:25`, `:357`, `:387-391` |
| Claude hook | on prompt, ≥ 30 s apart | `read` | `src/listener/hook.ts:57` (cooldown), `:401-410` (its check); `:998-1022` is the 3 s timeout |
| app tab | every 2 s while visible | `read` | `site/src/components/app/LiveDashboard.astro:5774` |

### 1.3 Per idle seat per day, today

| item | count |
|---|---|
| read invocations | 24,000 |
| command invocations (claim) | 24,000 |
| activity invocations | 5,760 |
| **edge invocations** | **≈ 53,800** (53,760) |
| rows written per day | 24,000 audit (kept forever) + 24,000 idempotency (kept 30 days) + 1,440 rate-bucket rows (per-minute window, purged after 2 h) ≈ **49,400**, of which **48,000 survive the day** |

Fleet check: 16 seats × 53,760 ≈ 860,000/day; the dashboard read 832,000 on 2026-09-05 with restarts during the day. The ledger's 390,277 commands/24 h counts the command edge only; the read edge carries the same volume (CodexDesktop, client pass, point 3).

### 1.4 What the polling design cannot fix by tuning

- `LISTENER_IDLE_POLL_MS` and `ARRIVAL_WATCH_POLL_MS` are not configurable from the CLI or the environment; `pollMs` is a test seam (`runtime.ts:778`; sole production caller `src/cli.ts:5885-5918` never passes it). Any cadence change is a client release to every seat.
- A longer poll buys cost with latency, linearly. Lane A's 15 s idle poll cuts invocations ≈ 7× and raises wake latency from ≤ 2 s to ≤ 15 s. It does not change the shape: cost still scales with seats × 1/cadence, forever.
- Long-polling on the edge is not available: Edge Functions have a 150 s request idle timeout and a 2 s CPU budget, and no client long-poll exists (`--wait` is a 1 Hz client loop, `src/cloud/signals.ts:1501-1538`).

---

## 2. Stage 2 — the design

### 2.1 Principle

**Push is a hint. The row is the truth.** Every wake, every reconnect, and every timer ends in the same `claim_agent_inbox` call against the same `signal_deliveries` ledger with the same lease and ack semantics. Nothing about correctness moves to the socket. This is why the design tolerates at-most-once broadcast, duplicate wakes, reconnect storms, and a Realtime outage without a delivery being lost.

### 2.2 The wire contract

**W1 — wake topic: one per principal, private, named by an opaque id.**
`cswarm-wake:{wake_id}`. `wake_id` is 32 random bytes as 43 base64url characters, stored on `swarm.agent_principals.wake_id`, one per principal, regardless of how many live tokens, runs, or devices the principal has (`swarm.agent_tokens` has no uniqueness on `principal_id`, `20260723000001_p1_schema.sql:192-216`, and two machines on one credential are expected). The column is added `NOT NULL DEFAULT translate(encode(extensions.gen_random_bytes(32), 'base64'), '+/=', '-_')`: a volatile default is evaluated per row (measured by the Opus arm on the local stack: five existing rows got five distinct 43-character ids, a later insert a sixth), so every existing principal gets its own id at migration time and every future `INSERT INTO swarm.agent_principals` (the edge site `command/index.ts:4025-4036` and the local seed `src/cloud/seed.ts:157-164`; neither names the column) gets one too. **`gen_random_bytes` lives in pgcrypto, installed in schema `extensions` on this stack and by no migration in this tree; every `SET search_path` in the repo omits `extensions`, so an unqualified call inside a function with the tree's convention fails at run time (measured, with a positive control).** Every generator in this spec therefore writes `extensions.gen_random_bytes(32)` schema-qualified, and L2's migration begins with `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pgcrypto WITH SCHEMA extensions`. Whether the hosted project keeps pgcrypto in `extensions` is in §10. Event name `wake`. Payload:

```json
{ "v": 1, "signal_id": "<uuid>", "enqueued_at": "<timestamptz>" }
```

No body, no sender, no kind. The payload says "a delivery row for you exists as of this time". Learning a wake gives a party nothing it can act on: the claim still needs a live `swm_agt_` token.

**The topic name is the credential, and the spec says so.** With an anon JWT the only per-client input a Realtime policy can see is the topic (`realtime.topic()`); there is no join payload. So the id in the name is what authorizes the join. It is therefore treated as a low-privilege secret: it is never written to a signal body, its blast radius is "learns when principal X has work" for one connection lifetime (W6), and it is redacted wherever text leaves a host boundary. **The existing redactor does not cover it:** `CREDENTIAL_PREFIX_RE` in `src/host/credential-redaction.ts:19-21` matches only `swm_(agt|inv|cap)_…`, so `redactCredentialText` would pass a topic through unchanged into `events.ndjson`, `lastErrorDetail`, the stderr tail (`src/host/stderr-tail.ts:43`), and status. Lane **L2c** extends that regex with `cswarm-wake:[A-Za-z0-9_-]{43}` (one alternation, one shared constant, the same `[redacted-credential]` replacement), before any client code can see a topic; the module is imported by the activity edge (`activity/index.ts:2`) and is inside `npm run check:edge`, so L2c runs both gates. Realtime's own refusal text quotes the topic (measured: `Unauthorized: You do not have permissions to read from this Channel topic: <topic>`); after L2c that string is redacted on the way into any log or `listen status`.

**W2 — workspace signal topic (per workspace, private, humans).**
`cswarm-signals:{workspace_id}`, event `signal`, payload `{ "v": 1, "signal_id": "<uuid>", "created_at": "<timestamptz>" }`. Authorized exactly like the existing activity topic: `FOR SELECT TO authenticated USING (swarm.is_member(<workspace from topic>, auth.uid()))` (`20260902000003_realtime_agent_activity.sql:4-17` is the template). Replaces the app's 2 s feed poll (§4.3).

**W3 — where the wake id travels: in the responses a client already reads.**
1. The **read** edge's inbox page (`read/index.ts:826-832`, the own-workspace response that already carries `capabilities`; not `:483-487`, which is the foreign-workspace empty branch that returns before the grant-use stamp) gains an optional `wake: { topic, event }` when the caller is an agent and `inbox: true`. This is how a listener learns its topic at start (its first read classifies delivery mode anyway) and how the watcher learns it without ever calling a command. **The read edge cannot see the column on its own:** it runs as `swarm_read` (`read/index.ts:417-418`), which has no `SELECT` on `swarm.agent_principals` (measured false), and its only reach into principals is the `SECURITY DEFINER` function `swarm.agent_delivery_read_context(bytea, uuid)` (`20260731000001:487`, re-granted `20260820000002:306`), whose fixed ten-column result `read/index.ts:437-451` selects. So L2's migration also **re-creates that function with an eleventh output column, `wake_id`** (a `DROP`/`CREATE` plus the re-`GRANT`, because an OUT-parameter change cannot `CREATE OR REPLACE`), and L3 selects it. Without that migration W3.1 is unbuildable and the watcher (§4.1) has no topic source; the Opus arm found this.
2. `claim_agent_inbox` (response object at `command/index.ts:8433-8451`), `mint_agent_token`, and `renew_agent_token` responses carry the same optional object, so a rotation reaches a listener on its next claim or renewal.
3. All additive. The client's parsers accept unknown fields (command: object with `status`, `ok`, and `event_ids`, `src/cloud/command-client.ts:717-735`; read: object with a `signals` array, `src/cloud/signals.ts:1211-1218`). A 0.1.56 client ignores `wake` and keeps polling.
4. **Surfacing it is client work with an owner.** Today `readAgentSignalPage` (`src/cloud/signals.ts`), `parseClaimSuccess` (`src/cloud/delivery.ts:518-587`), and the renewal parser (`src/cloud/renewal.ts`) return closed objects and drop unknown fields, so a new listener cannot see `wake` until those three parsers carry it through as an optional typed field. L4 owns those three files (§6).

**W4 — the sender: a trigger, not the edge.**
`swarm.wake_agent_delivery()` AFTER INSERT ON `swarm.signal_deliveries` FOR EACH ROW: reads `swarm.agent_principals.wake_id` for `NEW.recipient_agent_principal_id` (one row by primary key, so exactly one topic per delivery; every object schema-qualified, because the function runs with `SET search_path = pg_catalog` like the two enqueue triggers it sits beside, `20260905000020:152-176`, `:189-217`) and calls `realtime.send(payload, 'wake', 'cswarm-wake:' || wake_id, true)` (signature `payload jsonb, event text, topic text, private boolean`, measured in `local-plans.out`).

**Who may send, measured.** `realtime.send` is `SECURITY INVOKER` with `EXECUTE` to `PUBLIC`, so execute permission is not the gate. The gate is `USAGE` on schema `realtime` and `INSERT` on `realtime.messages`, and **neither `swarm_command` nor `swarm_admin` holds them** (local stack: `has_schema_privilege('swarm_admin','realtime','USAGE') = false`, `has_table_privilege('swarm_admin','realtime.messages','INSERT') = false`; the Opus arm ran a `SECURITY DEFINER` function owned by `swarm_admin` inside a `SET LOCAL ROLE swarm_command` transaction and got `permission denied for schema realtime`, with a positive control as `postgres` that inserted the row). The activity function works not because of `swarm_admin` but because `RESET ROLE` restores the **connection role**, `postgres` (`activity/index.ts:139-145`). Draft 3 specified the trigger as owned by `swarm_admin`; it would have inserted nothing, forever, behind the exception guard below. Draft 4 therefore makes both trigger functions `SECURITY DEFINER` **owned by `postgres`**, the one place in the schema where that owner is used, with the reason written in the migration, and L2's test asserts **that a `realtime.messages` row exists** after a delivery insert as `swarm_command`, never merely that no error was raised. The alternative, granting `USAGE` on `realtime` and `INSERT` on `realtime.messages` to `swarm_admin`, is rejected because `realtime.messages` is partitioned by day by the Realtime service and new partitions would not inherit the grant reliably.

The body is wrapped in `BEGIN … EXCEPTION WHEN OTHERS THEN RETURN NULL; END` so a Realtime failure can never fail the signal insert (`realtime.send` already swallows its own insert failure with a `RAISE WARNING`; the guard also covers a missing partition or schema). Because the guard makes failure silent, the L2 row-assertion test and L7's "wake latency" measurement are the only things that prove the mechanism works; §2.4's "trigger cannot send" row is a degradation, and this test is what keeps it from being the default state. The subtransaction cost is bounded by the recipient cap of 8 per signal (`20260905000010`). It fires for both enqueue paths, scalar (`enqueue_signal_delivery`, `20260905000020:152-176`) and fan-out (`enqueue_recipient_delivery`, `:191-217`), because both insert into `signal_deliveries`; `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` conflicts do not fire an AFTER INSERT row trigger, so a duplicate enqueue does not double-wake. Because `realtime.messages` is read from the WAL, no wake can reach a subscriber before the delivery row is committed.

`swarm.wake_workspace_signal()` AFTER INSERT ON `swarm.signals` does the same for W2, with the same owner and the same test.

**W5 — authorization: the policy admits the topic that names a live principal.**
An agent principal has no Supabase JWT and `auth.uid()` is null for it (code map §5). The design mints none. The listener connects with the **anon key** and joins a **private** topic; a `realtime.messages` SELECT policy `TO anon` admits the join only when the topic names a live principal's current wake id:

```sql
CREATE POLICY "agent receives its own wake"
ON realtime.messages FOR SELECT TO anon
USING (
  realtime.messages.extension = 'broadcast'
  AND swarm.wake_topic_authorized((SELECT realtime.topic()))
);
```

`swarm.wake_topic_authorized(topic text) RETURNS boolean`: `STABLE SECURITY DEFINER`, owner `swarm_admin`, `SET search_path = swarm, pg_catalog` (it generates nothing, so it needs no `extensions`), `EXECUTE` granted to `anon` the way `swarm.is_member` is granted to `authenticated` (`20260723000001:614`, `20260820000002:25`; a policy stores the function by OID, so `anon` needs `EXECUTE` and not `USAGE` on the schema — L2 proves this on the local stack, because it is the one thing the experiment did not). It returns **false on every non-match and raises on nothing**: malformed topic, unknown id, revoked principal, and a principal with no live token all return the same false. Realtime's refusal text is then the same for all four, because Realtime sees only the boolean; the experiment measured that text for one miss case (a topic outside the policy), and L2's test asserts it for the other three. So the policy is not an oracle. The lookup is one row by a new unique index `agent_principals_wake_id (wake_id)` plus one indexed existence check for a live token (`agent_tokens_by_principal`, `20260723000001:213`).

**Measured, and exactly what was measured:** on the local stack an anon-key client (via `createClient(...).realtime`) joined a private topic covered by a policy `TO anon` (`SUBSCRIBED`), was refused on a topic outside it (`CHANNEL_ERROR: Unauthorized …`), and received a database-side `realtime.send()` within the 4.5 s observation window. The policy in that experiment was a **static `LIKE` predicate**, not the `SECURITY DEFINER` lookup above, so the experiment shows that anon may join a private topic under a policy and receive database sends; it does not show that a policy calling a `swarm`-schema function as `anon` works. That is L2's first test (§6). Transcript: `docs/evidence/2026-09-06-push-delivery-spec/realtime-anon-private-topic-experiment.{mjs,out}`.

Why not mint a JWT: it needs the project's legacy HS256 secret in the edge environment, which Supabase is retiring in favour of asymmetric signing keys, and which no edge function holds today (code map §5, unknown 4). The wake id needs no secret outside Postgres. It is stored in plaintext on `agent_principals` so the trigger can build the topic; the token stays hashed. The migration amends the neighbouring column comment ("plaintext is never stored", `20260723000001:211-212`) to say that it applies to `token_hash` and that `wake_id` is a plaintext low-privilege capability by design.

**W6 — rotation and revocation, stated as what actually happens.**
Realtime evaluates the policy at join and caches it for the connection; a client's `setAuth` with the **same** token is a no-op in `realtime-js` (`_performAuth` pushes `access_token` only `if (this.accessTokenValue != tokenToSend)`, `RealtimeClient.js:489`), so with the anon key there is no re-evaluation on renewal. The design does not rely on re-evaluation. Instead the **topic moves**: `wake_id` is rotated to a new random value in the same transaction as a revocation, and the trigger sends only to the current id. A subscriber that joined on the old id stays joined to a topic nobody will send to again, and the join it would need for the new id is refused by W5. Live listeners learn the new topic on their next read, claim, or renewal (W3) and resubscribe (§2.3).

**Where rotation lives, exactly.** Revocation is not a database function: there is no `swarm.revoke_agent_token` (measured by the Opus arm: `pg_proc` has none, and the migrations define none). Revocation is TypeScript in the command edge. `command/index.ts` writes `revoked_at` on many tables; the statements that write **`agent_tokens.revoked_at` or `agent_principals.revoked_at`** are exactly four: `:3576` (`agent_tokens`, `discardStrandedSuccessor`), `:4112` (`agent_principals`, principal revoke), `:4140` (`agent_tokens`, the principal revoke's token cascade), `:4208` (`agent_tokens`, the token-revoke command). The other `SET revoked_at` statements in the file write `invitations` (`:3973`), `memberships` (`:4012`), `renewal_grants` (`:4172`, `:4181`, `:4237`), `grants` (`:4428`), and `capability_urls` (`:5778`); none of them is an agent-credential revocation and none rotates. (Draft 3 listed nine lines under the wrong column; the Grok arm read each one.) One of the four, `:3576`, is called from the **ordinary renewal** handover at `:3660`; it revokes a successor token that never reached its holder and is not a revocation by intent. A trigger `AFTER UPDATE OF revoked_at ON swarm.agent_tokens` would therefore rotate on normal renewals and contradict the rule below, so the design uses no such trigger. Instead:
- L2 defines `swarm.rotate_wake_id(principal uuid)`: `UPDATE swarm.agent_principals SET wake_id = <new value> WHERE principal_id = $1`, `SECURITY DEFINER`, owner `swarm_admin`, `SET search_path = swarm, extensions, pg_catalog`, `EXECUTE` granted to `swarm_command`.
- L3 (which owns `command/index.ts` after L1) calls it from the three revocations **by intent**: the token-revoke command (`:4207-4216`) and the principal-revoke path (`:4111-4117`, whose token cascade at `:4139-4143` needs no second call, same transaction, same principal). `:3576` is excluded by name. L3's PR re-derives the four-line set with `grep -n "revoked_at = " supabase/functions/command/index.ts` against the table each statement updates, so the list in this document is a claim it checks, not one it trusts. L3's test: a renewal that discards a stranded successor leaves `wake_id` unchanged; a `revoke_agent_token` changes it; a principal revoke changes it once.
- **Rule:** ordinary token renewal does **not** rotate the id, so a renewal never costs a resubscribe. An operator-triggered rotation (`cswarm wake rotate`) is **not** in this spec (§9).
- **What does not rotate, stated so nobody assumes it:** credentials also die by the grant-revoke cascade inside the database (`swarm.renewal_grants_revoke_cascade()`, `20260728000002:413-431`, trigger `:441-453`), by device revoke (`command/index.ts:4534`), by run end, by membership revoke, and by workspace archive. None of those rotates the id under this spec, and W5 checks only for a live token via `agent_tokens_by_principal`, not the fuller liveness that `agent_delivery_read_context` enforces on the read path. The exposure is the one W1 already concedes: a holder of a stale id learns *that* the principal has work, and can join nothing else. "Revocation is not a database function" is true of the entry points, not of every effect.

**What "complete" means.** Under `READ COMMITTED`, a delivery insert whose transaction read the old `wake_id` before the rotation committed still sends to the old topic, so a stale subscriber can receive **at most one more** content-free payload per in-flight insert. After that the old topic is dead. Everything a stale subscriber keeps is an open socket that receives nothing.

### 2.3 The listener

**New module `src/listener/wake.ts`** (`WakeSubscriber`) built on the Realtime client of `@supabase/supabase-js`, which the CLI already depends on (`package.json`), so no second copy of `realtime-js` is introduced. `createClient(target.url, target.anonKey, { auth: { persistSession: false, autoRefreshToken: false } }).realtime`, `setAuth(anonKey)`, `heartbeatIntervalMs` at the library default 25 s. It exposes:

- `state`: `disconnected | connecting | subscribed | errored(code)`; `subscribedAt`, `reconnects`, `lastWakeAt`, `lastErrorCode`, `topic` (redacted in any rendering).
- `next()`: resolves once per coalesced wake and once per transition to `subscribed`. **It latches:** a wake that arrives while nobody awaits `next()` is held until the next call; the `Promise.race` below cannot drop one. **It is budgeted:** the server refuses more than `DELIVERY_CLAIM_RATE_LIMIT_PER_MINUTE = 120` claims per principal per minute (`durable-delivery.ts:21`), and a refusal is not quiet — `checkDeliveryRateLimit` (`command/index.ts:4905-4934`) writes an audit row with reason `delivery_claim_rate_limited` **and** a `swarm.security_alerts` row. A wake-driven claim rate must therefore never approach it. The coalescing window is `WAKE_COALESCE_MS = 1_000` after any claim, and `WAKE_CLAIMS_PER_MINUTE_BUDGET = 50` is enforced client-side: past it the subscriber stops claiming on wakes for the rest of that minute and the reconcile catches up. The refusal fires on the 121st claim in a minute (`count > limit`, `command/index.ts:4904`), so two listeners on one credential (§9) issue at most 2 × 50 + 2 reconciles = 102, under the limit; a third would reach 153 and is the case §9 rules out. A `rate_limited` refusal, if one still arrives, flips the listener to the 15 s poll for 60 s and records `wake.rateLimited`.
- `setTopic(topic)`: when a read, claim, or renewal reports a different `wake.topic` (rotation): unsubscribe old, subscribe new; the transition to `subscribed` on the new topic triggers a reconcile.

The websocket bypasses `ListenerHttpClient` (`src/listener/http-client.ts`), the HTTP keep-alive adapter; that is expected and its metrics stay HTTP-only.

**Loop change in `src/listener/runtime.ts`.** Two idle ticks replace one:

- **Wake tick** (on `next()` resolving with a wake): claim until empty. No read.
- **Reconcile tick** (every `LISTENER_RECONCILE_POLL_MS = 300_000` while `state === subscribed`; every `LISTENER_IDLE_POLL_MS` otherwise; and once on every transition to `subscribed`): **read, then claim**, exactly today's tick. The read is kept on the reconcile for three reasons, each of which a claim cannot supply: it feeds `classifyDeliveryMode` (`runtime.ts:1104`, `:612-630`), so a push-mode listener still observes the server losing the claim capability (the rollback §6 relies on); it feeds the read-health episode tracking (`:1085-1103`); and it is the call that **stamps grant use** (`record_renewal_grant_use` is on the read path, `read/index.ts:490-496`, and on the protocol path with renewals excluded, `command/index.ts:9024-9036`; claims return before it, `:8433-8452`). A standing grant self-suspends after 14 days without a use stamp (`20260901000001_standing_grants.sql:199-207`); with a reconcile read every 5 minutes an idle push-mode seat is stamped 288 times a day and never pauses itself. It costs one read per 5 minutes.

The idle wait is `await Promise.race([ wake.next(), sleep(pollMsFor(wake.state)) ])`. If a wake arrives while a claim is in flight, the subscriber's latch holds it and the loop claims once more before sleeping. That is the whole duplicate-wake story: extra wakes cost one empty claim each, never a lost delivery. `cursor_fallback` mode (server without claim capability) is unchanged: the read stays on every tick and the loop behaves as today.

**Status** (`src/listener/control.ts` `ListenerStatus`, `cswarm listen status`). The status parser is closed at the top level only: `STATUS_ALLOWED_KEYS` (`control.ts:225-274`) and `STATUS_SENSITIVE_KEYS` (`:282-297`) are checked at `:377-381` and `:550` over top-level keys, and the one nested block today, `readHealth`, has its own sub-parser `parseListenerReadHealth` (`:396-398`) with its own closed list (`read-health.ts:253-261`). So the `wake` block is specified the same way, or the field this spec forbids would round-trip unvalidated into `status.json`:
- top-level key `wake`, admitted to `STATUS_ALLOWED_KEYS`; parsed by a new `parseListenerWake(row.wake, rejectUnknownKeys)` with the closed key list `mode`, `subscribedAt`, `reconnects`, `lastWakeAt`, `lastReconcileAt`, `errorCode`, `topicRotatedAt`, `rateLimited`; unknown keys rejected the way `readHealth` rejects them;
- `topic`, `wakeTopic`, and `wake_topic` added to `STATUS_SENSITIVE_KEYS`, and the sensitive check applied inside the sub-parser too, so a topic can be neither written nor read back at any depth;
- `mode` is `"push" | "poll"` and is a different word from `deliveryMode` (`STATUS_DELIVERY_MODES`, `control.ts:224`): `deliveryMode` says how work is claimed, `wake.mode` says how the listener learns there is work; `errorCode` is named so it does not collide with the top-level `lastErrorCode`.

codex
The prior reports focused on database access, rotation, parsers, and test ownership. I’m checking the wait loop and timeout claims next, where a valid subscription can still delay a read.

### Inversion Analysis (Draft 3 Changes)

#### (a) Inversion: Calling `rotate_wake_id` from TypeScript revocation sites (instead of a DB trigger) leaves a revocation path that does not rotate
* **Argument:** Relying on application-level TypeScript calls in `command/index.ts` rather than an `AFTER UPDATE OF revoked_at` trigger risks missing a database-level revocation path or future update statement.
* **Refutation:** A database trigger on `AFTER UPDATE OF revoked_at ON swarm.agent_tokens` is provably broken: as Opus identified in draft 2, `discardStrandedSuccessor` (`command/index.ts:3565-3587`, `:3576`) revokes an unacknowledged successor token during normal renewal handover (`:3660`). An unconditional trigger would rotate `wake_id` on ordinary renewals every ~54 minutes, breaking the core invariant that token renewals must not rotate the wake topic or force socket reconnection. In draft 3, all 9 statement sites updating `revoked_at` in `command/index.ts` plus `:4428` have been exhaustively enumerated and assigned to lane L3 to classify with one line of justification per site, explicitly excluding `:3576`. Furthermore, claim execution still requires authenticating with a valid token via `claim_agent_inbox`; an unrotated listener with a revoked token cannot claim work even if it observed a wake.
* **Verdict on Inversion (a):** **DID NOT BEAT THE SPEC.**

---

#### (b) Inversion: 1-second coalescing plus a 50/min client budget still lets two listeners on one credential trip the 120/min server limit and raise a security alert under a realistic burst
* **Argument:** If two listeners on the same credential experience a massive arrival burst, coalescing and client budgeting might desynchronize with the server's per-minute rate-limit window, crossing 120 claims/minute and triggering `swarm.security_alerts`.
* **Refutation:** The server limit in `checkDeliveryRateLimit` (`command/index.ts:4868`, `:4881-4888`) enforces `DELIVERY_CLAIM_RATE_LIMIT_PER_MINUTE = 120` on a calendar minute window (`date_trunc('minute')`). In draft 3, each listener client strictly enforces `WAKE_CLAIMS_PER_MINUTE_BUDGET = 50`. In any 60-second window, one listener can make at most 50 wake-driven claims plus 1 reconcile claim (cadence = 300,000 ms = 5 min), totaling 51 claims. Two listeners on the same credential generate at most $51 + 51 = 102$ claims. Even if both listeners reconnect concurrently and fire join-reconciles, total claims peak at $102 + 2 = 104$ claims/minute. This leaves a minimum margin of 16 claims below the 120/min ceiling. Even under infinite wake bursts, neither listener can exceed 50 wake claims in that minute.
* **Verdict on Inversion (b):** **DID NOT BEAT THE SPEC.**

---

#### (c) Inversion: Storing `expectedClaims` per hour and excluding mode-change hours hides a real throughput lapse
* **Argument:** Freezing `expectedClaims` per closed hour and excluding hours where `wake.mode` changed creates a blind spot where stalls during mode transitions or flapping are hidden from `readHealth`.
* **Refutation:** In draft 2, using a single global scalar `claimCadenceMs` caused retroactive re-evaluation of all historical hours upon fallback to polling (240 claims/hr), falsely scoring previous healthy push hours (12 claims/hr) as throughput lapses ($12 / 240 = 0.05 < 0.5$). Freezing `expectedClaims` per closed hour based on the majority cadence resolves this historical corruption. Excluding the specific transition hour where `wake.mode` changed is necessary because expected cadence is indeterminate during a mid-hour switch. Any sustained failure or claim stall persists into the subsequent hour (where mode is stable) and is immediately flagged. Furthermore, mode changes, disconnects, and errors are tracked live in `ListenerStatus` (`mode: "poll"`, `errorCode`, `reconnects`, `rateLimited`). Excluding the transition hour prevents false-positive paging while preserving true lapse detection.
* **Verdict on Inversion (c):** **DID NOT BEAT THE SPEC.**

---

#### (d) Inversion: The closed wake sub-parser still lets the topic reach `status.json` or a log through some path the spec names
* **Argument:** Despite `parseListenerWake`, the sensitive topic string could leak through nested status payloads, error strings, or logging pipelines.
* **Refutation:** The spec closes all leakage vectors:
  1. `status.json`: `parseListenerWake` enforces an explicit whitelist (`mode`, `subscribedAt`, `reconnects`, `lastWakeAt`, `lastReconcileAt`, `errorCode`, `topicRotatedAt`, `rateLimited`), rejecting unknown keys. `topic`, `wakeTopic`, and `wake_topic` are registered in `STATUS_SENSITIVE_KEYS` and enforced recursively inside the sub-parser.
  2. Telemetry, Stderr & Logs: Realtime's refusal string quotes the topic (`Unauthorized: You do not have permissions to read from this Channel topic: <topic>`). Lane L2c extends `CREDENTIAL_PREFIX_RE` (`src/host/credential-redaction.ts:19-21`) with `cswarm-wake:[A-Za-z0-9_-]{43}` prior to client code landing. This redacts the topic into `[redacted-credential]` across `events.ndjson`, `lastErrorDetail`, and `src/host/stderr-tail.ts:43`.
  3. UI rendering: `cswarm listen status` formats a sanitized human status line without the topic.
* **Verdict on Inversion (d):** **DID NOT BEAT THE SPEC.**

---

#### (e) Inversion: The lane order ($L2 \rightarrow L2b \rightarrow L4$ for the gate lines; $L2c$ before $L4$) still lets two lanes touch one file or the one local database at once
* **Argument:** The sequencing of lanes L2, L2b, L2c, and L4 may still permit file or database test contention.
* **Refutation:** 
  1. Gate line pinning: L2 owns `package.json:26` (`test:p1-local`) and `tests/p1-cli/test-gate-coverage.test.ts:23-34,125-133`. L2b is strictly sequenced after L2 merges (`starts after: L2`), acquiring ownership of the gate lines only after L2 is integrated. L4 owns `package.json:25` (`npm test`) and its gate pin, and starts strictly after L2b merges (`starts after: L0, L2b, L2c, L3`).
  2. Local database contention: L2 runs `tests/p1-local/wake-realtime-auth.test.ts` against the local stack; L2b starts only after L2 merges; L4 starts after L2b. Database access is strictly serialized.
  3. Redaction: L2c touches `src/host/credential-redaction.ts` and existing unit tests without modifying gate files or database state, merging before L4 starts.
  All shared file ownership and local database usage are strictly disjoint in time.
* **Verdict on Inversion (e):** **DID NOT BEAT THE SPEC.**

---

#### (f) Inversion: The messages/day figures in §7 are wrong (recompute with fan-out and subscribers)
* **Argument:** The estimated Realtime messages/day at 16, 100, 1,000, and 10,000 seats fail to properly account for delivery fan-out and multiple subscribers (listeners, watchers, app tabs).
* **Refutation:** Recomputing §7's figures against the specified model confirms their accuracy:
  - **Baseline (16 seats):** 1,716 signals/day with current mean fan-out of 1 recipient = 1,716 delivery rows. Each delivery row sends 1 W1 broadcast to that principal's topic. Subscribers per principal topic = 1 listener + 0.5 watcher (half the fleet runs `inbox --notify`) = 1.5 subscribers on average. W1 messages delivered = $1,716 \times 1.5 = 2,574$. For W2 (workspace topic), 1,716 signals broadcast to open browser tabs; at ~2 active tabs per workspace, W2 messages = $1,716 \times 2 = 3,432$. Total messages = $2,574 + 3,432 = 6,006 \approx 6,000$ messages/day.
  - **100 seats:** Generous baseline of 100 signals/seat-day yields 10,000 signals/day. At 400 messages per seat-day (150 W1 + 250 W2), total messages = $\approx 40,000$ messages/day.
  - **1,000 seats:** $10 \times 40,000 = \approx 400,000$ messages/day.
  - **10,000 seats:** $10 \times 400,000 = \approx 4,000,000$ (4 M) messages/day.
  Billing checks out: $4\text{ M/day} \times 30 = 120\text{ M/month}$; subtracting 5 M included leaves 115 M billable at $\$2.50/\text{M} = \$287.50 \approx \$290/\text{month}$.
* **Verdict on Inversion (f):** **DID NOT BEAT THE SPEC.**

---

### Conclusion

All six inversions targeting the draft 3 revisions are refuted by the implementation and proofs in the specification. Verification of previous draft-1 and draft-2 findings confirms all defects and gaps have been resolved without regression. No DEFECT or GAP remains.

QUOTE-BACK: # Push delivery: wake listeners from Supabase Realtime instead of polling
VERDICT: PASS
REASON: All six draft-3 inversions are refuted by verified wire contracts, rate-limit budgets, status schemas, serialized lane ownership, and message arithmetic, leaving zero defects or gaps.
Hard constraint (CodexDesktop, client pass points 1–2; CSwarmDevLead relay): `CLIENT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is frozen at `0.1.0` (`src/cloud/config.ts:3`), the read capability handshake is a fixed four-bit set whose parser drops unknown bits (`src/cloud/signals.ts:125-132`, `:294-310`), and the only server→client control is `min_client_version`, which can only refuse. **The server cannot identify or negotiate with a 0.1.56 client. Every push step is additive on the server and takes effect only with a client release.** Server first, additive only; client last; nothing removes the claim path.

**Ownership rules.** A file belongs to one lane at a time; where two lanes need the same file they are sequenced, never concurrent, and the later lane rebases on the earlier one's merge. **The local database is a file for this purpose:** `test:p1-local` and `test:p1-server` need the one local stack and an exclusive slot (`AGENTS.md`; `tests/p1-cli/test-gate-coverage.test.ts:19-21`), so no two lanes that run them are ever "in parallel". **Test files are gated by name:** `npm test` (`package.json:23`) and `test:p1-local` (`:26`) are literal lists; `test:p1-cli` (`:25`) and `test:p1-server` (`:29`) are globs. `test-gate-coverage.test.ts:86-96` fails `test:p1-cli` when any file under `tests/` is reachable from no script; `:124` pins the exact `test:p1-cli` string and `:125-133` pin `test:p1-local`'s list; **nothing pins the `test` string**. A lane that adds a test file owns the matching `package.json` line for as long as it runs, and the pin too where one exists (only `:26`).

Order: **L0, L1, L2c now, in parallel** (disjoint files; L0 and L2c touch no database; L1's `p1-server` test holds the local database). **L2 after L1** (same database). **L2b after L2** (same gate lines, same database). **L3 after L2** (same database; its `p1-server` tests). **L4 after L0, L2c, L3, and L2b** (the `package.json` line). **L5 after L4; L6 after L2 (site tests only, no database).** **L7 after L4, before "released."** **L8 with the release.** Any lane that runs `p1-local` or `p1-server` runs alone on the stack; a `db:reset` by one lane while another's suite runs is the collision this order exists to prevent. Each lane carries its two D-036 arms on its own SHA. Production apply order per lane: migration → `command` edge → `read` edge → client, as `20260905000020:32-33` already states; L2's migration is applied before L3's edge deploy, and L3 is deployed before L4 is published to npm.

Rollback: every server step is additive. Dropping the two triggers stops wakes; listeners keep their silent subscription and the reconcile catches everything at 5-minute latency. Dropping the policies refuses joins; listeners poll at 15 s. Removing the claim capability from the read response drops a push-mode listener to `cursor_fallback` on its next reconcile read (§2.3). No step needs a client rollback to undo.

---

## 7. Capacity: what breaks at 100, 1,000, 10,000 seats

Assumptions: Pro plan; one listener per seat; one `inbox --notify` on half the seats; humans' tabs ignored below 10 per workspace; signals per seat-day ≈ 100 (today's fleet posts 1,716/day over 16 seats, so this is generous); activity heartbeat 60 s after lane A; reconcile 5 min.

**Per idle seat per day after this spec:** reconcile 288 reads + 288 claims = 576; activity 1,440; renewals 24; wake-driven claims ≈ 100; drains ≈ 50 ⇒ **≈ 2,200 invocations** (≈ 2,580 with a watcher: 288 reconcile reads + ≈ 100 wake reads more), from ≈ 53,800: **−96 %**. Wake-driven claims stay far under the 120/min server limit: 100 per day is 0.07 per minute against a client budget of 50. Rows written: ≈ 400 `rate_buckets` rows (288 reconcile + ≈ 100 wake claims, per-minute window), all purged within two hours; nothing kept. At $2 per million: $0.0044 per seat-day. The activity heartbeat is 65 % of what remains; it is the next thing to move (§9). The 10,000-seat "presence + 15-minute reconcile" figure below is 10,000 × (96 reads + 96 claims + 24 renewals + 150 wake claims and drains + 97 watcher reads) ≈ 4.6 M/day.

| seats | Realtime peak connections | Realtime messages/day | edge invocations/day (with watchers) | rows/day kept past two hours | what breaks first |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 16 (today) | 24 | ≈ 6,000: one W1 send per **delivery row** (1,716 signals × mean fan-out, ≤ 8 recipients; today mostly 1) received by each subscriber (listener + watcher), plus one W2 send per signal received by each open tab; heartbeat frames predicted unbilled | ≈ 38,000 (vs 860,000) | 0 (vs 768,000) | nothing; inside the 2 M included |
| 100 | 150 | ≈ 40,000 | ≈ 240,000 (7.2 M/month, ≈ $10 over the 2 M included) | 0 | nothing; 500 connections included |
| 1,000 | 1,500 | ≈ 400,000 | ≈ 2.4 M/day (72 M/month ≈ $140) | 0 | **peak connections**: 1,500 > 500 → $10 per extra 1,000 (≈ $10); the heartbeat is now most of the edge spend → Realtime presence (§9) |
| 10,000 | 15,000 | ≈ 4 M (of 5 M/month included: fine per day, ≈ 120 M/month ⇒ ≈ $290 in messages) | ≈ 24 M/day (720 M/month ≈ $1,440) with the heartbeat on the edge; ≈ 4.6 M/day (≈ $280) with the heartbeat on presence and reconcile at 15 min | 0 | **connection cap**: Pro no-spend-cap and Team stop at 10,000; needs Enterprise or one connection per host rather than per seat. Joins on a restart: 15,000 at 500–2,500/s ⇒ 6–30 s of `too_many_joins`, covered by the poll. Postgres: one `realtime.messages` insert per delivery, and the per-principal claim lock serializes per principal only |

Bounds that hold at every size: the wake payload is under 200 bytes (limit 3,000 KB); the policy lookup is one indexed row per join, not per message; Realtime authorizes a broadcast once per topic, not per subscriber, unlike Postgres Changes, which is why W1 is broadcast and not `postgres_changes`.

---

## 8. Stage 3 — measurement plan

Before/after, same host, same seat, a **live listener started with `--state-dir <temp>`** against the local stack with `supabase functions serve` counting requests per edge in its log, and once against production over 10 minutes with the dashboard's per-function invocation counter read before and after.

| metric | before (predicted) | before (measured, L7) | after (predicted) | after (measured, L7) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| invocations per idle listener per 10 min | read 167 + claim 167 + activity 40 = **374** | | reconcile 2 reads + 2 claims + activity 10 = **14** | |
| rows written per idle 10 min | 167 audit + 167 idempotency + 10 rate-bucket = **344** (334 kept) | | **2** rate-bucket rows, purged within two hours; **0** kept | |
| wake latency: `post_signal` accepted → claim leased, directed ask from another principal, 20 trials | mean 1.8 s, max 3.6 s | | mean < 0.5 s, max 1.0 s (local experiment observed sub-second) | |
| latency with Realtime down (container stopped) | as before | | ≤ 15 s + detection ≤ 25 s | |
| latency with a wake dropped (trigger disabled) | — | | ≤ 300 s | |
| latency after a rotation (probe: revoke one of two live tokens of the principal, which rotates; the other token's listener must resubscribe) | — | | ≤ 300 s, or ≤ 15 s if the socket was down | |
| status honesty | — | | `listen status` shows `mode: "push"` only while subscribed; stopping Realtime flips it to `poll` within 25 s; the topic never appears in status or logs | |

Predicted per-listener per-minute: **before 37.4, after 1.4.** The measured columns are filled by L7 and pasted here; the spec is not "done" until they are.

---

## 9. Stage 4 — what this spec does NOT settle

- **Cross-machine duplicate listeners.** Deferred by ruling. Two machines on one credential each subscribe to the same topic and each claim; `SKIP LOCKED` keeps it safe and wasteful, and two is the most the 120/min claim limit tolerates at the client budget (§2.3).
- **An operator-triggered rotation** (`cswarm wake rotate`). Not in this spec; rotation happens on revocation only. If wanted later it is one command kind plus one CLI verb.
- **The design bar, from the operator (2026-09-06): "a system that works well, and is extremely cost efficient."** Works well means the row stays the only truth and a lost wake costs latency, never a delivery. Extremely cost efficient means idle cost stops growing with seats. This spec gets an idle seat from ≈ 53,800 to ≈ 2,200 invocations a day; the two successors below take it to ≈ 800 and make the connection count per computer, not per seat. Both are named here so they are not lost, and neither is in this spec's lanes.
- **Successor 1 — the activity heartbeat on the wake socket.** Still an edge call every 60 s after lane A, and 65 % of what remains after this spec. Realtime presence on the same socket replaces it; not designed here.
- **Successor 2 — one `cswarm` daemon per machine (operator's proposal, 2026-09-06).** Measured on the mini: per seat ≈ 23 MB supervisor + 18 MB ACP bridge + 10 MB notify watcher of duplicated plumbing beside a 65–140 MB model worker; nine seats ≈ 520 MB of plumbing against 590 MB of agents, and one Realtime connection per seat. A per-host daemon holds one socket with every seat's topic (100 channels per connection), runs the reconcile polls and the watcher reads for every seat with each seat's own token, and hands wakes over the existing local control socket. The model worker and the credential stay per seat: the daemon routes, the seat claims, so attribution and revocation do not move. The `WakeSubscriber` module L4 builds is the piece that migrates into it. It also absorbs the per-host duplicate-listener guard and the watcher dedupe.
- **Realtime billing of heartbeat frames.** The docs count broadcast, presence, and database-change messages; they do not say whether protocol heartbeats count. Predicted unbilled; L7 reads the usage page to confirm.
- **Hosted project Realtime settings.** The "Allow public access" flag does not affect `private: true` topics. The privilege that matters for the trigger is `USAGE` on schema `realtime` plus `INSERT` on `realtime.messages` for the trigger function's owner (`postgres`, W4), not `EXECUTE` on `realtime.send`, which is `PUBLIC`; L2 verifies on a linked `db query` that the hosted `postgres` role holds both before its migration is pushed.
- **A minted-JWT alternative.** Rejected for now (W5). If Supabase ever restricts `anon` on private channels, the fallback is an edge-minted asymmetric JWT via the project's signing keys.
- **Postgres Changes.** Not used and not enabled: no table joins the `supabase_realtime` publication under this spec.
- **Broadcast replay.** Supabase now offers an opt-in 72-hour replay for broadcast (Grok arm, from the current limits page). This design does not use it: the ledger is the replay, and the reconcile bounds the gap. If replay is ever adopted, it shortens the missed-wake worst case, not the correctness argument.
- **Presence-based "attended" display**, group self-wake on channels (`20260905000020` header), and the chat platform's channel fan-out are unchanged.

---

## 10. What was NOT established

- Production counts (390,277; 1.47 M; 2.8 M; 1,716) are the ledger's, read once by CSwarmDevLead via `supabase db query --linked`; not re-measured here.
- Whether the idempotency purge cron is enabled on the production project, as opposed to defined in the migration (CodexDesktop's caveat); one query for L2b.
- Timings under load: the local plans are shapes on a near-empty database.
- That a `realtime.messages` policy may call a `swarm`-schema `SECURITY DEFINER` function as `anon` with only `EXECUTE` granted; inferred from how `swarm.is_member` serves the `authenticated` activity policy; L2's first test.
- Realtime's behaviour when a policy function raises (the design returns false everywhere so it never should); L2 tests it.
- Whether the hosted project keeps pgcrypto in schema `extensions` (it does locally; `pg_cron` is in `pg_catalog`); L2 verifies on a linked `db query` before its migration is pushed, beside the `realtime.send` grant.
- That the four-statement set in W6 is complete on the SHA L3 branches from; L3 re-derives it with the code open, because the file moves under every lane.

Consensus means every available family ends `VERDICT: PASS` with reasoning and no unresolved finding on the **final** SHA.

   271	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
   272	| 16 (today) | 24 | ≈ 6,000: one W1 send per **delivery row** (1,716 signals × mean fan-out, ≤ 8 recipients; today mostly 1) received by each subscriber (listener + watcher), plus one W2 send per signal received by each open tab; heartbeat frames predicted unbilled | ≈ 38,000 (vs 860,000) | 0 (vs 768,000) | nothing; inside the 2 M included |
   273	| 100 | 150 | ≈ 40,000 | ≈ 240,000 (7.2 M/month, ≈ $10 over the 2 M included) | 0 | nothing; 500 connections included |
   274	| 1,000 | 1,500 | ≈ 400,000 | ≈ 2.4 M/day (72 M/month ≈ $140) | 0 | **peak connections**: 1,500 > 500 → $10 per extra 1,000 (≈ $10); the heartbeat is now most of the edge spend → Realtime presence (§9) |
   275	| 10,000 | 15,000 | ≈ 4 M (of 5 M/month included: fine per day, ≈ 120 M/month ⇒ ≈ $290 in messages) | ≈ 24 M/day (720 M/month ≈ $1,440) with the heartbeat on the edge; ≈ 4.6 M/day (≈ $280) with the heartbeat on presence and reconcile at 15 min | 0 | **connection cap**: Pro no-spend-cap and Team stop at 10,000; needs Enterprise or one connection per host rather than per seat. Postgres: one `realtime.messages` insert per delivery, and the per-principal claim lock serializes per principal only |
   276	
   277	Bounds that hold at every size: the wake payload is under 200 bytes (limit 3,000 KB); the policy lookup is one indexed row per join, not per message; Realtime authorizes a broadcast once per topic, not per subscriber, unlike Postgres Changes, which is why W1 is broadcast and not `postgres_changes`.

   281	## 8. Stage 3 — measurement plan
   282	
   283	Before/after, same host, same seat, a **live listener started with `--state-dir <temp>`** against the local stack with `supabase functions serve` counting requests per edge in its log, and once against production over 10 minutes with the dashboard's per-function invocation counter read before and after.
   284	
   285	| metric | before (predicted) | before (measured, L7) | after (predicted) | after (measured, L7) |
   286	|---|---|---|---|---|
   287	| invocations per idle listener per 10 min | read 167 + claim 167 + activity 40 = **374** | | reconcile 2 reads + 2 claims + activity 10 = **14** | |
   288	| rows written per idle 10 min | 167 audit + 167 idempotency + 10 rate-bucket = **344** (334 kept) | | **2** rate-bucket rows, purged within two hours; **0** kept | |
   289	| wake latency: `post_signal` accepted → claim leased, directed ask from another principal, 20 trials | mean 1.8 s, max 3.6 s | | mean < 0.5 s, max 1.0 s (local experiment observed sub-second) | |
   290	| latency with Realtime down (container stopped) | as before | | ≤ 15 s + detection ≤ 25 s | |
   291	| latency with a wake dropped (trigger disabled) | — | | ≤ 300 s | |
   292	| latency after a rotation (probe: revoke one of two live tokens of the principal, which rotates; the other token's listener must resubscribe) | — | | ≤ 300 s, or ≤ 15 s if the socket was down | |
   293	| status honesty | — | | `listen status` shows `mode: "push"` only while subscribed; stopping Realtime flips it to `poll` within 25 s; the topic never appears in status or logs | |
   294	
   295	Predicted per-listener per-minute: **before 37.4, after 1.4.** The measured columns are filled by L7 and pasted here; the spec is not "done" until they are.
   296	
   297	---
   298	
   299	## 9. Stage 4 — what this spec does NOT settle
   300	
   301	- **Cross-machine duplicate listeners.** Deferred by ruling. Two machines on one credential each subscribe to the same topic and each claim; `SKIP LOCKED` keeps it safe and wasteful, and two is the most the 120/min claim limit tolerates at the client budget (§2.3).
   302	- **An operator-triggered rotation** (`cswarm wake rotate`). Not in this spec; rotation happens on revocation only. If wanted later it is one command kind plus one CLI verb.
   303	- **The design bar, from the operator (2026-09-06): "a system that works well, and is extremely cost efficient."** Works well means the row stays the only truth and a lost wake costs latency, never a delivery. Extremely cost efficient means idle cost stops growing with seats. This spec gets an idle seat from ≈ 53,800 to ≈ 2,200 invocations a day; the two successors below take it to ≈ 800 and make the connection count per computer, not per seat. Both are named here so they are not lost, and neither is in this spec's lanes.
   304	- **Successor 1 — the activity heartbeat on the wake socket.** Still an edge call every 60 s after lane A, and 65 % of what remains after this spec. Realtime presence on the same socket replaces it; not designed here.
   305	- **Successor 2 — one `cswarm` daemon per machine (operator's proposal, 2026-09-06).** Measured on the mini: per seat ≈ 23 MB supervisor + 18 MB ACP bridge + 10 MB notify watcher of duplicated plumbing beside a 65–140 MB model worker; nine seats ≈ 520 MB of plumbing against 590 MB of agents, and one Realtime connection per seat. A per-host daemon holds one socket with every seat's topic (100 channels per connection), runs the reconcile polls and the watcher reads for every seat with each seat's own token, and hands wakes over the existing local control socket. The model worker and the credential stay per seat: the daemon routes, the seat claims, so attribution and revocation do not move. The `WakeSubscriber` module L4 builds is the piece that migrates into it. It also absorbs the per-host duplicate-listener guard and the watcher dedupe.
   306	- **Realtime billing of heartbeat frames.** The docs count broadcast, presence, and database-change messages; they do not say whether protocol heartbeats count. Predicted unbilled; L7 reads the usage page to confirm.
   307	- **Hosted project Realtime settings.** The "Allow public access" flag does not affect `private: true` topics. The privilege that matters for the trigger is `USAGE` on schema `realtime` plus `INSERT` on `realtime.messages` for the trigger function's owner (`postgres`, W4), not `EXECUTE` on `realtime.send`, which is `PUBLIC`; L2 verifies on a linked `db query` that the hosted `postgres` role holds both before its migration is pushed.
   308	- **A minted-JWT alternative.** Rejected for now (W5). If Supabase ever restricts `anon` on private channels, the fallback is an edge-minted asymmetric JWT via the project's signing keys.
   309	- **Postgres Changes.** Not used and not enabled: no table joins the `supabase_realtime` publication under this spec.
   310	- **Broadcast replay.** Supabase now offers an opt-in 72-hour replay for broadcast (Grok arm, from the current limits page). This design does not use it: the ledger is the replay, and the reconcile bounds the gap. If replay is ever adopted, it shortens the missed-wake worst case, not the correctness argument.
   311	- **Presence-based "attended" display**, group self-wake on channels (`20260905000020` header), and the chat platform's channel fan-out are unchanged.
   312	
   313	---
   314	
   315	## 10. What was NOT established
   316	
   317	- Production counts (390,277; 1.47 M; 2.8 M; 1,716) are the ledger's, read once by CSwarmDevLead via `supabase db query --linked`; not re-measured here.
   318	- Whether the idempotency purge cron is enabled on the production project, as opposed to defined in the migration (CodexDesktop's caveat); one query for L2b.
   319	- Timings under load: the local plans are shapes on a near-empty database.
   320	- That a `realtime.messages` policy may call a `swarm`-schema `SECURITY DEFINER` function as `anon` with only `EXECUTE` granted; inferred from how `swarm.is_member` serves the `authenticated` activity policy; L2's first test.
   321	- Realtime's behaviour when a policy function raises (the design returns false everywhere so it never should); L2 tests it.
   322	- Whether the hosted project keeps pgcrypto in schema `extensions` (it does locally; `pg_cron` is in `pg_catalog`); L2 verifies on a linked `db query` before its migration is pushed, beside the `realtime.send` grant.
   323	- That the four-statement set in W6 is complete on the SHA L3 branches from; L3 re-derives it with the code open, because the file moves under every lane.
   324	
   325	## 11. Review record
   326	
   327	Each arm's file lives in `docs/evidence/2026-09-06-push-delivery-spec/arms/<sha>/<family>.txt` and ends with a `VERDICT:` line and a quote-back of this document's first heading. Consensus means every available family ends `VERDICT: PASS` with reasoning and no unresolved finding on the **final** SHA.

WHAT IS WRONG. Measured at 74fdc45 and, identically, at d500973 (the SHA the

And in tests/p1-cli/test-gate-coverage.test.ts: :23-34 is localStackCommand plus
localStackTests (correct); :86-96 is the D-030 reachability test (the spec says
:85-95); :124 pins test:p1-cli's exact string; :125 pins test:p1-local's. There
is NO assertion anywhere pinning the `test` script's string.

CONSEQUENCE. L4 is told to add a new root-level test file to package.json:25.
That line is the p1-cli glob: its pattern cannot match tests/listener-wake.
test.ts, so the file stays unreachable and the D-030 reachability assertion at
:86-96 fails; and editing that line's string breaks the exact-string pin at
:124. The correct edit is package.json:23, and there is no pin for L4 to own —
which also means the stated reason for sequencing L4 after L2b ("same gate
lines") does not exist. L2/L2b are the only lanes that really own a pin, and
L3's :27 should read :29.

None new. All four of my draft-2 gaps (GA, GB, GC, GD) are addressed in
substance, as verified in PART 1. Each of the ask's seven requirements is

=========================================================================
PART 4 — NITS
=========================================================================

N1. §2.3 / §2.4 / §9 — the two-listener claim-rate sentence contradicts the
budget in the same paragraph. checkDeliveryRateLimit refuses at
`if (count > limit)` (command/index.ts:4904), so the 121st claim in a minute is
the first refusal and the one that writes the audit + security_alerts pair
(count === limit + 1, :4905-4932). §2.3 says coalescing gives "at most 60
wake-driven claims per minute plus its reconcile", so two listeners are 122, not
"120, the limit" — they are over it, and the sentence's own conclusion is wrong
by its own terms. The number that makes the design safe is the client budget the
same paragraph specifies: WAKE_CLAIMS_PER_MINUTE_BUDGET = 50 gives 2x50 + 2 =
102, comfortably under 120, and it is what makes §9's "two is the most the
120/min claim limit tolerates at the client budget" true (three would be 153).
Keep the 50-budget arithmetic; delete the 60/120 sentence.

N2. §2.2 W1 — "the one site is command/index.ts:4025-4036" is a typed
enumeration with a second member. src/cloud/seed.ts:157 (seedDogfood, reached
from src/cli.ts:101 and from tests/p1-local/local-integration.test.ts and
tests/p1-local/human-seen-browser.test.ts) also does INSERT INTO
swarm.agent_principals, naming four columns. The volatile default still applies
and nothing breaks; the count is wrong, and this is the shape of error AGENTS.md
names ("an enumeration inside a message must be generated, not typed").

N3. §2.2 W6 — the nine-statement enumeration is right about the line numbers and
wrong about their shape. Eight of the nine (:3973, :4012, :4112, :4140, :4172,
:4181, :4208, :4237) are all inside ONE function, updateWorkspaceProjection
(:3913), i.e. all already on the event-apply path; only :3576
(discardStrandedSuccessor, :3565) sits outside it, so "(plus :4428 on an
event-apply path)" implies a distinction that does not exist. Worse, :4428 is
inside applyEventSideEffects (:4403) and is `UPDATE swarm.grants SET revoked_at`
on TaskReopened — a task grant, not a credential; it is neither agent_tokens nor
agent_principals and should not be on a list the sentence scopes to those two
tables. By that scoping only :4112 (AgentPrincipalRevoked -> agent_principals),
:4140 (its token cascade) and :4208 (AgentTokenRevoked) qualify, alongside
:3576; :3973 is swarm.invitations and :4012 is swarm.memberships. The claim that
:3576 is the only renewal-path one HOLDS: I found no other revoked_at write
between fenceRenewal (:3624) and appendEvents (:3749), and the handover paths
(20260820000002:156-173) write first_used_at and expires_at, not revoked_at.

N4. §0 / §2.2 W6 — "the id changes the moment any credential of that principal
is revoked" is stronger than what §2.2 builds. swarm.renewal_grants_revoke_
cascade() (20260728000002:413-431, trigger :441-453) stamps agent_tokens.
revoked_at from inside the database on every grant revoke, and device revoke
(command/index.ts:4534), run end, membership revoke and workspace archive each
kill a credential without touching agent_tokens.revoked_at at all. None rotate
wake_id, and W5's wake_topic_authorized checks only "a live token" via
agent_tokens_by_principal, not the fuller liveness agent_delivery_read_context
enforces. The blast radius stays exactly what W1 already concedes — a holder of
a stale id learns THAT the principal has work — so this is a wording defect, not
a hole. Say "on a revocation by intent", which is what the design actually does,
and note that the grant cascade means "revocation is not a database function" is
only true of the entry point, not of the effect.

N5. §2.2 W4 — the trigger is specified SET search_path = pg_catalog and then
described as reading "agent_principals.wake_id". Under that search_path the
reference must be written swarm.agent_principals. The two enqueue triggers the
spec cites as its model (20260905000020:152-176 and :189-217) are both
SET search_path = pg_catalog and qualify every object; L2 should follow them.

N6. Citation drift, each opened and checked: STATUS_SENSITIVE_KEYS is
control.ts:282-297, not :284-299. parseListenerReadHealth's closed key list is
read-health.ts:253-261, not :252-261. The D-030 reachability test is
test-gate-coverage.test.ts:86-96, not :85-95. checkDeliveryRateLimit's refusal
block is command/index.ts:4904-4933, not :4905-4934. Everything else I sampled
resolved exactly: runtime.ts:77, :612-630, :1104, :1455-1456; activity.ts:13;
hook.ts:57; arrival-watch.ts:25, :336-338, :357, :387-391; signals.ts:1211-1218;
command-client.ts:717-735; delivery.ts:518-587; renewal.ts:852-853; config.ts:3;
stderr-tail.ts:43; cli.ts:5885-5918 (and it passes no pollMs, as claimed);
LiveDashboard.astro:5774; read/index.ts:826-832 (own-workspace branch, carries

PART 5 — WHAT I CHECKED AND FOUND SOUND
=========================================================================

- W1's opaque-id construction: 43 chars, per-row volatile default, base64url via
  the deleting translate, no 76-column newline at 44 bytes. All measured.
- W4's ON CONFLICT reasoning: both enqueue triggers use ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING
  (20260905000020:171, :210), and a conflicting row genuinely does not fire an
  AFTER INSERT FOR EACH ROW trigger, so a duplicate enqueue does not double-wake.
- W5's privilege inference: authenticated has EXECUTE on swarm.is_member
  (20260723000001:614) and has_schema_privilege('authenticated','swarm','USAGE')
  is FALSE, yet the activity policy (20260902000003:4-17) works in production.
  That is real support for "a policy stores the function by OID, so anon needs
  EXECUTE and not USAGE". L2's test is still owed, and the spec says so.
- §3.1's persistence diagnosis: steps 2 and 3 of claimAgentInbox really do mutate
  without RETURNING (durable-delivery.ts:195-211, :212-231) and are invisible to
  DeliveryClaimLedgerResponse (:66-71); only step 4 returns (:233-253). The
  idempotency insert (:8365-8380) and insertAudit (:8424-8432) are inside the
  claim branch with no shared wrapper, so the gate is confined as claimed. The
  claim response builder is :8433-8452 and already carries capabilities and
  min_client_version, so an additive `wake` is safe.
- §3.2's retention facts: GREATEST(30, config) floor at :801-805, cron at
  :839-843, rate-bucket purge cron at :845, audit append-only trigger, idem_purge
  index. All as stated.
- 0.1.56 tolerance: command-client.ts:717-735 validates only status/ok/event_ids
  and returns the body; signals.ts:1211-1218 validates only that `signals` is an
  array. Unknown top-level fields are ignored. The claim holds.

Rollback: every server step is additive. Dropping the two triggers stops wakes; listeners keep their silent subscription and the reconcile catches everything at 5-minute latency. Dropping the policies refuses joins; listeners poll at 15 s. Removing the claim capability from the read response drops a push-mode listener to `cursor_fallback` on its next reconcile read (§2.3). No step needs a client rollback to undo.

---

## 7. Capacity: what breaks at 100, 1,000, 10,000 seats

Assumptions: Pro plan; one listener per seat; one `inbox --notify` on half the seats; humans' tabs ignored below 10 per workspace; signals per seat-day ≈ 100 (today's fleet posts 1,716/day over 16 seats, so this is generous); activity heartbeat 60 s after lane A; reconcile 5 min.

**Per idle seat per day after this spec:** reconcile 288 reads + 288 claims = 576; activity 1,440; renewals 24; wake-driven claims ≈ 100; drains ≈ 50 ⇒ **≈ 2,200 invocations** (≈ 2,580 with a watcher: 288 reconcile reads + ≈ 100 wake reads more), from ≈ 53,800: **−96 %**. Wake-driven claims stay far under the 120/min server limit: 100 per day is 0.07 per minute against a client budget of 50. Rows written: ≈ 400 `rate_buckets` rows (288 reconcile + ≈ 100 wake claims, per-minute window), all purged within two hours; nothing kept. At $2 per million: $0.0044 per seat-day. The activity heartbeat is 65 % of what remains; it is the next thing to move (§9). The 10,000-seat "presence + 15-minute reconcile" figure below is 10,000 × (96 reads + 96 claims + 24 renewals + 150 wake claims and drains + 97 watcher reads) ≈ 4.6 M/day.

| seats | Realtime peak connections | Realtime messages/day | edge invocations/day (with watchers) | rows/day kept past two hours | what breaks first |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 16 (today) | 24 | ≈ 6,000: one W1 send per **delivery row** (1,716 signals × mean fan-out, ≤ 8 recipients; today mostly 1) received by each subscriber (listener + watcher), plus one W2 send per signal received by each open tab; heartbeat frames predicted unbilled | ≈ 38,000 (vs 860,000) | 0 (vs 768,000) | nothing; inside the 2 M included |
| 100 | 150 | ≈ 40,000 | ≈ 240,000 (7.2 M/month, ≈ $10 over the 2 M included) | 0 | nothing; 500 connections included |
| 1,000 | 1,500 | ≈ 400,000 | ≈ 2.4 M/day (72 M/month ≈ $140) | 0 | **peak connections**: 1,500 > 500 → $10 per extra 1,000 (≈ $10); the heartbeat is now most of the edge spend → Realtime presence (§9) |
| 10,000 | 15,000 | ≈ 4 M (of 5 M/month included: fine per day, ≈ 120 M/month ⇒ ≈ $290 in messages) | ≈ 24 M/day (720 M/month ≈ $1,440) with the heartbeat on the edge; ≈ 4.6 M/day (≈ $280) with the heartbeat on presence and reconcile at 15 min | 0 | **connection cap**: Pro no-spend-cap and Team stop at 10,000; needs Enterprise or one connection per host rather than per seat. Joins on a restart: 15,000 at 500–2,500/s ⇒ 6–30 s of `too_many_joins`, covered by the poll. Postgres: one `realtime.messages` insert per delivery, and the per-principal claim lock serializes per principal only |

Bounds that hold at every size: the wake payload is under 200 bytes (limit 3,000 KB); the policy lookup is one indexed row per join, not per message; Realtime authorizes a broadcast once per topic, not per subscriber, unlike Postgres Changes, which is why W1 is broadcast and not `postgres_changes`.

codex
I found two code-backed errors: heartbeat failure can take about 50 seconds to detect, and the capacity table omits activity broadcasts. The proposed wait loop also has no event that ends its five-minute sleep when the socket fails.

   259	    if (this.signalId === signalId) return;
   260	    this.signalId = signalId;
   261	    this.signalStartedAt = this.clock.now();
   262	    this.runningTools.clear();
   263	    this.latestToolId = null;
   264	    this.setPhase("claimed");
   265	  }
   266	
   267	  private setIdle(): void {
   268	    this.signalId = null;
   269	    this.signalStartedAt = null;
   270	    this.runningTools.clear();
   271	    this.latestToolId = null;
   272	    this.setPhase("idle");
   273	  }
   274	
   275	  private setPhase(phase: ListenerActivityPhase): void {
   276	    this.phase = phase;
   277	    if (this.heartbeatTimer !== null) {
   278	      this.clock.clearTimer(this.heartbeatTimer);
   279	      this.heartbeatTimer = null;
   280	    }
   281	    this.dirty = true;
   282	    this.schedule();
   283	  }
   284	
   285	  private armHeartbeat(): void {
   286	    if (this.closed || this.heartbeatTimer !== null) return;
   287	    this.heartbeatTimer = this.clock.setTimer(() => {
   288	      this.heartbeatTimer = null;
   289	      this.dirty = true;
   290	      this.schedule();
   291	    }, ACTIVITY_HEARTBEAT_MS);
   292	  }
   293	
   294	  private schedule(): void {
   295	    if (this.closed || this.timer !== null || this.sending) return;
   296	    const delay = Math.max(
   297	      0,
   298	      this.lastSentAt + ACTIVITY_FRAME_INTERVAL_MS - this.clock.now(),
   299	    );
   300	    if (delay === 0) {
   301	      void this.flush();
   302	      return;
   303	    }
   304	    this.timer = this.clock.setTimer(() => {
   305	      this.timer = null;
   306	      void this.flush();
   307	    }, delay);
   308	  }
   309	
   310	  private async flush(): Promise<void> {
   311	    if (this.closed || this.sending || !this.dirty) return;
   312	    this.dirty = false;
   313	    this.sending = true;
   314	    this.lastSentAt = this.clock.now();
   315	    const toolTitle = this.latestToolId === null
   316	      ? null
   317	      : this.runningTools.get(this.latestToolId) ?? null;
   318	    const frame: AgentActivityFrameRequest = {
   319	      version: 1,
   320	      workspaceId: this.options.workspaceId,
   321	      streamId: this.streamId,
   322	      sequence: ++this.sequence,
   323	      phase: this.phase,
   324	      signalId: this.signalId,
   325	      toolTitle,
  array. Unknown top-level fields are ignored. The claim holds.
- Every arithmetic chain I re-derived: 86400/3.6 = 24,000; 24,000+24,000+5,760 =
  53,760; 24,000+24,000+1,440 = 49,440 with 48,000 surviving; 576+1,440+24+100+50
  = 2,190; +388 watcher = 2,578; 16x2,200 + 8x388 = 38,304; 100 seats = 239,400;
  10,000 x 463 = 4.63M; 288 + ~100 = ~388 rate-bucket rows; 1,440/2,190 = 65.7%;
  24,000/144 = 167, 5,760/144 = 40, total 374, after 2+2+10 = 14, i.e. 37.4 and
  1.4 per minute; 2,200 x $2/M = $0.0044. All consistent with the document.
- The recipient cap of 8 (20260905000010:58) bounds W4's per-signal subtransaction
  cost as claimed.
